## 🤖 ChatGPT

The contracts you provided have terms regarding cancellation, refunds, and discontinuing services:

1. **Abundant Servicing Agreement**:
   - **Cancellation and Refunds**: The contract states that it "cannot be canceled or rescinded by Client for any reason" and that all payments are non-refundable【7†source】.
   - **Billing and Services Discontinuation**: If there is a default on payment, the servicer (Abundant Servicing, LLC) has the right to accelerate the payment, making the entire remaining balance due within five business days. Additionally, services can be terminated or suspended if payments are not made【7†source】.

2. **Merchant Contract with Luvelle Luxe LLC (BNB Syndicate Program)**:
   - **Refunds or Cancellations**: The contract does not explicitly mention provisions for refunds or cancellations outside of payment terms. It highlights the total payment and terms but does not specify conditions under which you could terminate services for a refund【8†source】.

Given these terms, both contracts appear to have limited provisions for cancellation and refunds, specifically stating non-refundable terms or lacking clear refund clauses. If the company's inability to secure funding (as promised) is not addressed in the contracts, this could be a basis for dispute, potentially requiring mediation or legal action as outlined under the dispute resolution sections.

## 👤 You

So how can I cancel and stop

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

To pursue cancellation and stop further payments despite restrictive terms in the contracts, consider the following steps:

1. **Document Failure of Promised Services**: Since the funding aspect was a critical promise made in the sales call, gather all records of this promise, including any emails, texts, or call notes. This helps establish a case that the contract terms were not fully met by the provider.

2. **Invoke Dispute Resolution**:
   - Both contracts outline a **dispute resolution process**. Begin by formally notifying both companies (Abundant Servicing and Luvelle Luxe LLC) in writing about the failure to fulfill promised funding. Request a meeting or mediation to discuss canceling the contract based on this breach.
   - Cite the specific promise of funding and explain how its failure impacts your ability to continue with the program, making it ineffective or irrelevant for your needs.

3. **Demand for Termination Due to Breach**:
   - Reference the lack of promised funding as a material breach of the agreement. While the contracts restrict cancellation, a material breach (failure to perform a promised, essential aspect) can sometimes override standard non-cancellation terms.
   - Indicate that, due to their inability to fulfill critical elements of the service, you are terminating the contract and halting future payments. Be specific, polite, and firm in this communication.

4. **Stop Payment and Notify Your Bank**:
   - If no resolution is achieved, contact your bank to stop payments. Inform them about the disputed charges, detailing the breach and failed services.
   - **Credit Card Dispute**: If you’re paying by credit card, initiate a dispute with your card issuer. Present your evidence (sales call promises, correspondence) to argue for a chargeback due to unfulfilled services.

5. **Prepare for Legal Action if Needed**:
   - Should the companies pursue payment, you may need to consult a consumer rights or contract attorney to contest the agreements based on the unfulfilled promises made. Legal grounds can include material misrepresentation or breach of implied terms based on promises made during the sales process.

Taking these steps ensures you’ve followed all contractual and formal channels, giving you a stronger position if the matter escalates.

---

**[Your Name]**  
**[Your Address]**  
**[Your Email Address]**  
**[Date]**

**[Company Name - Abundant Servicing, LLC / Luvelle Luxe LLC]**  
**[Company Address or Contact Email]**

Subject: **Formal Notice of Contract Termination and Refund Request Due to Non-Performance**

Dear [Contact Name/Customer Service Department],

I am writing to formally address an issue regarding our agreement, executed on [Date], for the [Program Name] with your company. My understanding was that this program would encompass specific support and resources to secure funding for my business activities, as emphasized in your sales call prior to my commitment.

Upon my enrollment, I was led to expect that securing funding was a core component of the value provided under this agreement. However, it has come to my attention that this service, which is central to my ability to leverage the program effectively, has not been delivered. This constitutes a material breach of our agreement, as the fulfillment of this key service was a primary reason for my involvement and continued payments under the contract.

Given this failure to meet essential expectations as represented in your sales communications, I formally request the following:

1. **Immediate Termination of Agreement**: As the terms of our agreement have not been met, I request that our contract be terminated immediately without penalty.
   
2. **Full Refund of Paid Amounts**: I request a refund for the payments made, as the lack of promised funding renders the program unworkable for my intended purpose.

3. **Cessation of All Further Billings**: Please cease any future payments or charges associated with this agreement effective immediately.

While I am prepared to resolve this matter amicably, I am committed to exercising my consumer rights if necessary, including formal dispute channels and any other legal options available should a mutual resolution not be achievable.

I expect your confirmation of the agreement termination and refund within **10 business days** of this letter. This timeframe reflects both the urgency of the matter and my intention to reach a fair conclusion swiftly.

Thank you for your immediate attention to this matter. I look forward to your prompt response to resolve this issue professionally.

Sincerely,  
**[Your Name]**

---

This letter presents your request clearly, emphasizes the breach, and includes a reasonable timeframe for response. By positioning your expectations firmly and professionally, you underscore the seriousness of the issue while keeping the door open for a cooperative resolution.

## 👤 You

what are the factual and legally upholding basis(s) to terminate the contract and get a full refund, and not negatively impact credit

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

To terminate the contract, secure a refund, and avoid negative credit consequences, you need to establish a **factual and legal basis** for your claims. Here's a breakdown:

---

### **Factual and Legal Grounds to Terminate the Contract**

1. **Material Breach of Contract**:
   - A **material breach** occurs when one party fails to perform a key obligation that is central to the purpose of the contract.
   - In this case:
     - The sales call promised services (e.g., securing funding) that were not delivered.
     - If funding was a critical component for your ability to benefit from the program, its absence undermines the agreement's purpose.
   - Legal Precedent: Courts often allow termination when the breaching party fails to provide what was promised, rendering the contract fundamentally ineffective.

2. **Misrepresentation or Fraudulent Inducement**:
   - If the sales call exaggerated or falsely promised outcomes (e.g., guaranteed funding) to induce you into signing, this may constitute **fraudulent misrepresentation** or **negligent misrepresentation**.
   - If you can prove that these promises were false and you relied on them to your detriment, the contract may be voidable.
   - Relevant Evidence: Recordings, emails, or texts from the sales process.

3. **Unconscionable Terms**:
   - A clause stating the contract "cannot be canceled or rescinded by Client for any reason" (as in your contract) may be deemed **unconscionable**:
     - Unfairly one-sided clauses are often struck down if they deny a reasonable ability to terminate, particularly when the other party fails to perform key services.
   - Legal Precedent: Courts may void clauses that leave no remedy for a consumer in cases of non-performance.

4. **Violation of Consumer Protection Laws**:
   - Many states have laws prohibiting unfair or deceptive business practices. If promises made during the sales process were knowingly false or misleading, they may violate these statutes (e.g., **FTC Act**, **state-level unfair trade practice laws**).
   - This provides grounds to rescind the contract and claim damages or refunds.

5. **Failure of Consideration**:
   - In contract law, both parties must receive consideration (value). If promised services (e.g., funding assistance) were not delivered, you may argue a **failure of consideration**, invalidating your obligation to pay.

---

### **Legal Strategies to Avoid Credit Impacts**

1. **Proactive Notification to Creditors**:
   - If payments are stopped, the servicer may attempt to report negative activity to credit bureaus. To prevent this:
     - Notify the servicing company (Abundant Servicing, LLC) in writing that the debt is disputed due to breach of contract.
     - Under the **Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA)**, creditors must mark disputed debts and cannot report them without verification.

2. **Request Verification**:
   - Under FCRA, if negative credit reporting occurs, you have the right to:
     - Dispute the entry with credit bureaus.
     - Demand verification from the creditor before they can report the debt as delinquent.

3. **Challenge Legal Enforceability of the Contract**:
   - If the terms are voided due to material breach, misrepresentation, or unconscionability, you can argue the debt is **not legally enforceable**.
   - Courts typically uphold credit reporting only for legally valid debts.

4. **Negotiate Conditional Settlement**:
   - As part of resolving the issue, negotiate a written agreement with the company stipulating:
     - No negative reporting to credit bureaus.
     - Withdrawal of any negative reports already made.

5. **Potential Legal Recourse Against Negative Reporting**:
   - If they report a disputed debt without verifying it or fail to note the dispute, you can sue under FCRA for damages and attorney’s fees.

---

### **Action Plan**

1. **Gather Evidence**:
   - Collect all documents, recordings, and communications related to the sales call and program promises.

2. **Send a Legal Demand Letter**:
   - State the basis for termination (material breach, misrepresentation, or unconscionability).
   - Demand confirmation of termination and a refund within 10 business days.
   - Include a formal warning against reporting negative credit activity.

3. **Stop Payments Through Legal Means**:
   - Notify your bank of the dispute and halt recurring payments.
   - Dispute charges with your credit card issuer if applicable.

4. **Consult an Attorney (If Needed)**:
   - For added leverage, have an attorney formalize your demand and threaten legal action under consumer protection laws if necessary.

---

This approach ensures a strong legal foundation for contract termination and minimizes credit risk, while emphasizing the company's obligations to resolve the matter fairly.

Dear [Relevant Contact Name(s)],  

I am writing to formally demand the immediate termination of all agreements associated with the [Program Name] I enrolled in on [Date], the cessation of any further charges, the issuance of a full refund for all payments made to date, and assurance that no negative credit reporting will be made. This demand is based on numerous material breaches of contract, misrepresentations, and violations of consumer protection principles.  

### **Factual Basis for Termination**  

1. **Funding Misrepresentation**:  
   - During the sales call, I was explicitly told that I could secure funding "within just a few days."  
   - It has now been **over 2–3 weeks**, and I have been informed by the funding department that I must wait an **additional 60 days to even begin** the process, which itself will take several more weeks to complete. This misrepresentation undermines the very foundation of my decision to enroll.  

2. **Misrepresentation of Authorized User Accounts (AUs)**:  
   - I was assured that I could purchase **multiple authorized user accounts (AUs)** at an affordable rate of **$100–$300** each, and that these accounts would remain on my credit profile **indefinitely**, with only a small recurring annual fee.  
   - After enrolling and signing the contract, I was instead quoted a **limit of 2 AUs**, each reporting for only **60 days**, at an approximate cost of **$500 per AU**. This drastic deviation is unacceptable.  

3. **Coaching Call Frequency**:  
   - I was explicitly told during the sales process that coaching calls would occur **twice a week**.  
   - After enrollment, I discovered that these calls are actually conducted only **once per week**, falling far short of the promised value.  

4. **Discrepancy in Agreement Amount**:  
   - I was assured during the sales call that my total agreement amount had been reduced to **$10,000** and that I could avoid any fees by paying that amount in full.  
   - However, the financing partner, **MyAbundant.com**, has stated that I owe **$13,000**, despite my understanding of the reduced amount and full payment terms.  

5. **Disjointed Agreements and Multiple LLCs**:  
   - The contracts reference **different LLCs** (Luvelle Luxe LLC, Abundant Servicing, LLC) with **separate terms and unclear integration**. This lack of transparency further complicates enforcement of the agreement and appears intentionally disjointed, raising concerns about compliance with consumer protection standards.  

---

### **Legal Grounds for Termination and Refund**

1. **Material Breach**:  
   - The repeated failure to deliver on core promises (funding, AU accounts, coaching call frequency, and total payment amount) constitutes a **material breach** of the agreement, rendering it voidable under contract law.  

2. **Misrepresentation and Fraudulent Inducement**:  
   - The misrepresentations made during the sales call, which I relied upon in entering into this agreement, meet the criteria for **fraudulent inducement**. These false statements invalidate my consent to the agreement and warrant termination.  

3. **Unconscionable Terms**:  
   - Clauses barring cancellation or refunds under any circumstances, even in cases of non-performance, are **unconscionable** and unenforceable under contract law.  

4. **Violation of Consumer Protection Laws**:  
   - These misrepresentations likely violate state and federal **unfair and deceptive practices laws** (e.g., the Federal Trade Commission Act).  

5. **Failure of Consideration**:  
   - The lack of promised services, funding, and compliance with verbal assurances constitutes a **failure of consideration**, nullifying my obligation to perform under the contract.  

---

### **Demands**

Given the above, I demand the following actions be taken immediately:  

1. **Termination of Contract**:  
   - Terminate all agreements between myself and your organizations.  

2. **Full Refund**:  
   - Issue a full refund of all payments made to date.  

3. **Cessation of Further Payments**:  
   - Cease all further billing, charges, or attempts to collect payments.  

4. **No Negative Credit Reporting**:  
   - Ensure that no negative credit reporting is made to any credit bureau. If any reporting has already occurred, initiate immediate removal and provide me with confirmation.  

5. **Written Confirmation**:  
   - Provide written confirmation of the above actions within **10 business days** of this letter.  

---

### **Notice of Legal Action**  

If I do not receive confirmation of these actions within the stated timeframe, I will have no choice but to escalate this matter through:  

- Filing formal complaints with the **Federal Trade Commission (FTC)** and the **Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB)**.  
- Pursuing legal remedies for breach of contract, misrepresentation, and violations of consumer protection laws, including recovery of damages and attorney’s fees.  

This letter is a formal and legally enforceable notice to resolve this matter promptly and professionally. I trust you will act in good faith to avoid further escalation.  

Sincerely,  
**[Your Full Name]**

This letter demands an immediate termination of my contracts, a full refund, and assurances of no negative credit reporting.  

---

### **The Hook: A Broken Promise with Far-Reaching Implications**

Imagine investing your trust, your time, and your money into a promise designed to empower you, only to find that the foundation of that promise was built on untruths. That is my experience with this program, and it is why I am compelled to write this letter.  

I entered into this agreement with the clear understanding—based on the sales call—that I would secure funding of **$100K+ within days**, enabling me to cover program costs and build my business. Instead, I’ve encountered delays, misleading terms, and a program structure that is fundamentally misaligned with what I was promised.  

These failures have not only placed undue financial strain on me but also invalidated the intent and purpose of this agreement. The very premise upon which I agreed to the terms is now untenable.  

---

### **Detailed Basis for Termination**

1. **Funding Promise Broken**:  
   - During the sales call, I was explicitly told I could secure **$100K+ in funding within days**.  
   - Weeks have passed with no funding. The funding department now informs me I must wait up to **60 additional days just to begin the process**, which itself takes weeks to complete. This timeline is entirely inconsistent with what was promised and has rendered the program unworkable for me.  

2. **Affordability and Reliance on Funding**:  
   - I cannot afford this program out of pocket. The only reason I entered into this agreement was the assurance of rapid funding, which would allow me to both pay for the program and execute the business strategies taught within it. Without this funding, continuing is financially impossible.  

3. **Misrepresentation of Authorized User Accounts (AUs)**:  
   - I was promised the ability to purchase **multiple AUs** at **$100–$300 each**, with **permanent reporting** and only a small recurring fee.  
   - Instead, I have been offered **a limit of 2 AUs**, at a cost of approximately **$500 each**, and with **only 60 days of reporting**—a material misrepresentation that undermines this program’s effectiveness.  

4. **Coaching Call Frequency**:  
   - Promised: **Twice per week.**  
   - Delivered: **Once per week.**  
   - This discrepancy further diminishes the value of the program.  

5. **Discrepancy in Total Agreement Cost**:  
   - Promised: A reduced agreement of **$10,000** with the ability to pay in full to avoid additional fees.  
   - Delivered: A total amount of **$13,000**, as stated by the financing partner, despite my full payment intent.  

6. **Disjointed and Conflicting Terms**:  
   - The contracts reference multiple LLCs with varying and unclear terms, including Luvelle Luxe LLC and Abundant Servicing, LLC. These inconsistencies raise serious concerns about compliance and enforcement.  

---

### **Legal Basis for Termination and Refund**

1. **Material Breach**: The failure to provide key promised services (e.g., funding, AU accounts, coaching calls) constitutes a material breach, voiding the contract.  

2. **Misrepresentation and Fraudulent Inducement**: False assurances regarding funding, AUs, and program costs invalidate my consent to the agreement.  

3. **Unconscionability**: The contract’s terms, which bar cancellation and refunds regardless of non-performance, are unconscionable and unenforceable.  

4. **Failure of Consideration**: Without the promised funding and services, the contract lacks the mutual consideration required for enforcement.  

5. **Consumer Protection Violations**: The misrepresentations likely violate state and federal laws prohibiting deceptive business practices.  

---

### **My Demands**

Given these issues, I demand the following actions:  

1. **Immediate Termination of All Agreements**: Terminate all contracts between myself and your organizations.  
2. **Full Refund**: Refund all payments made to date, totaling **[Amount Paid]**.  
3. **Cessation of Further Billing**: Cease all billing and collection activities immediately.  
4. **No Negative Credit Reporting**: Ensure no negative reporting to credit bureaus. If any such reporting has occurred, initiate its removal immediately.  
5. **Confirmation**: Provide written confirmation of these actions within **10 business days** of the date of this letter.  

---

### **TL;DR Conclusion**

This program was sold on promises of rapid funding, affordable AU accounts, and superior coaching—all of which have proven false. I cannot afford the program without the promised funding, which invalidates the agreement’s intent. I demand an immediate termination, a full refund, and assurances that my credit will not be negatively impacted.  

Failure to resolve this matter promptly will compel me to file formal complaints with the **FTC**, **CFPB**, and other consumer protection bodies, and to pursue legal action to recover my damages and enforce my rights.  

I trust you will act swiftly and in good faith to resolve this issue.  

Sincerely,  
**[Your Full Name]**

   - **What Was Said**: It was presented during the sales call that properties would be **provided to students**.  
   - **What Was Delivered**: After enrollment, I learned that students are required to **cold outreach** to landlords and negotiate agreements themselves, which was not disclosed upfront and is a significant deviation from my expectations.  

2. **Business Bank Account Setup Issues**:  
   - Following the **exact guidance** provided for applying for business bank accounts—reporting **$450K annual income** as **actual (not projected)**—resulted in multiple denials.  
   - This guidance was unrealistic for a new business with no established revenue and led to unnecessary complications in the account approval process.  

3. **Funding Timeline and Fees**:  
   - **What Was Said**: $100K+ in funding would be available within days, with fees capped at **3–5%**.  
   - **What Was Delivered**: Funding requires a **60+ day wait** to even begin, followed by weeks to complete, with fees quoted at **10%**, significantly higher than promised.  

4. **Authorized User (AU) Accounts**:  
   - **What Was Said**: Multiple AUs would be available at **$100–$300 each**, with **permanent reporting** and only a small recurring fee.  
   - **What Was Delivered**: I was offered **only 2 AUs**, costing approximately **$500 each**, with a reporting duration of only **60 days**.  

5. **Coaching Support**:  
   - **What Was Said**: Coaching calls would be held **twice per week** and provide sufficient support for student success.  
   - **What Was Delivered**: Only **one weekly group coaching call** is included, with no option for **1-on-1 coaching calls**, which are offered by comparable programs.  

6. **Cost Discrepancy**:  
   - **What Was Said**: My total agreement was reduced to **$10,000**, with an option to avoid fees by paying in full.  
   - **What Was Delivered**: The financing partner reflects a total cost of **$13,000**, which contradicts what was agreed upon during the sales call.  

7. **Program Transparency**:  
   - The contracts reference multiple LLCs, including **Luvelle Luxe LLC** and **Abundant Servicing, LLC**, with conflicting terms, creating confusion about the responsibilities and enforceability of the agreement.  

---

### **Legal Basis for Termination and Refund**

1. **Material Breach**:  
   - Misrepresentation of key services, timelines, fees, and program features constitutes a **material breach**, rendering the agreement voidable under contract law.  

2. **Fraudulent Inducement**:  
   - False claims about funding, competitor comparisons, and program features were used to induce me into signing. This invalidates my consent to the agreement.  

3. **Unconscionability**:  
   - Terms prohibiting cancellation or refunds despite clear failures in service delivery are **unconscionable** and legally unenforceable.  

4. **Failure of Consideration**:  
   - The absence of promised services and deliverables nullifies the mutual value exchange required for contract enforcement.  

5. **Consumer Protection Violations**:  
   - Misrepresentations likely violate federal and state laws against **unfair and deceptive trade practices** (e.g., the **FTC Act**).  

---

### **Request for Resolution**

I respectfully request the following actions:  

1. **Immediate Contract Termination**: Terminate all agreements between myself and your organizations.  
2. **Full Refund**: Refund all payments made to date, totaling **[Amount Paid]**.  
3. **Cessation of Further Billing**: Stop all billing and collection activities related to this program.  
4. **Credit Protection**: Ensure no negative credit reporting is made. If any such reporting has already occurred, initiate its removal immediately.  
5. **Confirmation**: Provide written confirmation of these actions within **10 business days** of the date of this letter.  

---

### **Fair Exchange for Resolution**  

In exchange for the termination of my contract and a full refund, I agree not to post any negative reviews, comments, or feedback about the program on any public platforms, including **forums, Reddit, social media, or review sites**. This ensures a mutually beneficial resolution that protects both parties' interests.  

---

### **Conclusion**  

I entered into this agreement in good faith based on representations made during the sales process. However, the discrepancies outlined above have made it impossible for me to proceed effectively under the current terms. I trust you will act promptly and professionally to resolve this matter.  

Please confirm the resolution of this matter in writing by **[specific date, 10 business days from today]**.  

Sincerely,  
**[Your Full Name]**  

---

- **AUs**: Included, with explicit comparison between what was promised and delivered.  
- **Funding**: Clearly stated discrepancies in timeline and fees.  
- **Property Process**: Fact-based clarification about the deviation between promises and reality.

A **typed name** is often sufficient for initial correspondence like this, especially if sent via email or an online platform, as it establishes your identity and intent. However, certain circumstances might require a **signature and date** for added legal formality, particularly if:  

1. **The contracts explicitly require written notice with a signature** for termination or disputes.  
2. **You plan to send this via certified mail or another formal delivery method**, where a signed document might carry more weight in court or legal proceedings.  

### Recommendation:  
1. **For Email**: A typed name is fine for now, especially if this is your first communication demanding resolution.  
   - Example:  
     Sincerely,  
     **[Your Full Name]**  
     **Without Recourse**  

2. **For Certified Mail or Legal Follow-Up**: Include a physical signature and date to formalize your demand.  
   - Example:  
     Sincerely,  
     [Signature]  
     **[Your Full Name]**  
     **Without Recourse**  
     **Date: [MM/DD/YYYY]**

### Why Signing and Dating Might Be Better:
- It demonstrates that the letter is a deliberate and formal communication.  
- It adds credibility and ensures there’s no ambiguity about your intent, especially if the matter escalates legally.  
- For certified mail, it strengthens your evidence trail for having made the request properly.  

If in doubt, adding a signature and date is a safe, legally secure choice.

Mentioning actions like filing complaints with regulatory bodies (FTC, CFPB, Attorney General) is **not considered threatening or extortion** as long as the language is factual, neutral, and doesn't include coercive or retaliatory intent. However, mentioning **public posting on social media, forums, blogs, or influencers** carries more risk. If it is framed as a way to pressure or coerce the other party into granting your demands, it could be misconstrued as extortion. Instead, you should avoid referencing public posting in your correspondence to ensure your communications remain professional and focused on legal avenues.

- **Regulatory Bodies (FTC, CFPB, Attorney General):**  
   Explicitly stating that you may file complaints with these bodies if the matter isn’t resolved is safe because it’s a formal, lawful process for dispute resolution.  
   - Example: *"If we cannot reach a resolution, I may explore filing formal complaints with relevant regulatory bodies such as the FTC, CFPB, and the Attorney General's office."*

- **Seeking Legal Advice:**  
   Mentioning that you may seek legal counsel is also appropriate.  
   - Example: *"Should the matter remain unresolved, I may consult legal professionals to explore additional steps to protect my rights."*

### **What to Avoid**
- **Public Exposure to Pressure Resolution:**  
   Mentioning social media, blogs, forums, or influencers could potentially be seen as coercive if framed as a means to force compliance. Even if your intention is genuine, it may appear retaliatory.

### **Alternative Approach**
If you feel public accountability is important, avoid mentioning it in the initial stages of communication. Focus on regulatory and legal actions, as these are neutral and protected avenues. You can later choose to take public steps if warranted, but it's best not to mention them preemptively in correspondence.

**Subject:** Final Request for Immediate Contract Termination and Refund  

Dear [BNB Syndicate Owner] and [MyAbundant Representative],  

I am following up to reiterate my formal request for the termination of my contract, a full refund of all payments made to date, and written confirmation that my credit will not be negatively impacted.  

This request stems from the failure to deliver key elements promised during the sales process, which constitute **fraudulent misrepresentation**, **material breach of contract**, and **unconscionable practices**. Below, I have outlined the core issues and supporting legal grounds for my request.  

---

### **Key Issues and Misrepresentations**  

1. **Failure to Deliver Promised Funding**:  
   - **What Was Promised**: $125K+ in 0% business funding secured within days to afford the program and initiate property arbitrage.  
   - **What Was Delivered**: After over a month of waiting, the funding process remains incomplete, with additional delays of 30–60 days and the likelihood of receiving only a fraction of the promised amount.  

2. **Misrepresentation of Program Features**:  
   - **Authorized User (AU) Accounts**: Promised multiple AUs at $100–$300 each with permanent reporting; delivered only 2 AUs costing ~$500 each, with reporting limited to 60 days.  
   - **Property Acquisition**: Promised that properties would be “provided” as part of the program; delivered instructions requiring independent cold outreach to landlords, a significantly different process.  

3. **Misleading Guidance**:  
   - Banking guidance to report $450K as "actual income" on applications for a newly formed LLC resulted in multiple denials, causing unnecessary complications.  

4. **Cost Discrepancies**:  
   - The agreement was reduced to $10,000 during the sales process, yet MyAbundant reflects a financing total of $13,000, contradicting what was explicitly stated.  

---

### **Legal Basis for Termination and Refund**  

1. **Material Breach of Contract**:  
   - The failure to deliver essential program elements—including funding, property acquisition support, and AUs—invalidates the enforceability of the contract.  

2. **Fraudulent Misrepresentation**:  
   - Pre-contract assurances regarding funding, fees, timelines, AUs, and property support induced me to sign the agreement. These statements, proven false, render my consent invalid.  

3. **Unconscionability**:  
   - Terms barring refunds or cancellations, despite the program’s inability to deliver, are legally unenforceable due to their one-sided nature.  

4. **Failure of Consideration**:  
   - The program failed to provide the agreed-upon services, nullifying the mutual value exchange required for enforcement of the contract.  

5. **Consumer Protection Violations**:  
   - Misrepresentations likely violate federal and state consumer protection laws, including the **FTC Act**, which prohibits unfair and deceptive trade practices.  

---

### **Proposed Resolution**  

To resolve this matter professionally and in good faith, I request:  

1. Immediate termination of all agreements related to the program and financing.  
2. A full refund of all payments made to date, totaling **[amount paid]**.  
3. Cessation of further billing or collection activities related to this program.  
4. Written confirmation that my credit will not be negatively impacted and no negative remarks will be made internally or externally.  

---

### **Consumer Rights and Platform Accountability**  

While I hope to resolve this matter directly, if resolution is not achieved, I may explore additional steps to ensure transparency and accountability, including:  

- Filing formal complaints with regulatory bodies such as the **Federal Trade Commission (FTC)**, the **Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB)**, and my **state Attorney General’s office**.  
- Reporting my experience to **Meta’s advertising integrity team**, as I encountered the program through their platform. Misleading or fraudulent advertising violates their policies, and I believe they would investigate the matter thoroughly.  

These actions are not intended to escalate unnecessarily but are steps available to me as a consumer under the law.  

---

### **Closing**  

I trust that you will review this matter fairly and consider this request in the spirit of professionalism. Please confirm receipt of this email and provide a resolution within **5 business days**. I am happy to provide any additional documentation needed to facilitate resolution.  

Thank you for your attention.  

Sincerely,  
**Alan Nguyen**  
**Without Recourse**

After reviewing the contracts and correspondence provided, here are the **factual and legally justifiable reasons** to nullify the agreements and demand a refund, with no impact on your credit. This response incorporates both the specific provisions in the contracts and legal doctrines that invalidate their enforcement under the circumstances.

---

### **Key Legal Points to Invalidate the Contracts**

#### **1. Fraudulent Misrepresentation**
   - **Verbal Promises During Sales Process:** Statements made during the sales process are legally actionable, particularly when they were a determining factor in your decision to sign. These include:
     - $125K+ in 0% business funding delivered within days .
     - Lower funding fees (3–5%), significantly different from the delivered 10% .
     - Properties being "provided" to students, as opposed to requiring personal cold outreach .

   - **Legal Doctrine:** Misrepresentations made before the contract was signed, even if not explicitly written in the agreement, constitute **fraudulent inducement**, invalidating your consent. This undermines the enforceability of the contract .

---

#### **2. Material Breach of Contract**
   - **Failure to Deliver Essential Elements:**
     - No funding has been delivered after over a month, with an estimated additional delay of 30–60 days. The amount likely to be secured is a small fraction of what was promised, making the program impractical for its intended purpose .
     - Guidance on reporting $450K in income led to repeated bank denials, showing that the guidance provided was impractical and unworkable .
   - **Legal Doctrine:** The failure to deliver key program elements constitutes a **material breach**, releasing you from your obligations under the agreement.

---

#### **3. Unconscionability**
   - **Terms Prohibiting Refunds or Cancellation:** The refusal to allow cancellation or refunds despite clear program failures is inherently unfair and one-sided, rendering such terms **unconscionable and unenforceable** .
   - **Legal Doctrine:** Courts often strike down terms that are excessively favorable to one party at the expense of the other.

---

#### **4. Failure of Consideration**
   - **Absence of Promised Deliverables:** Without the promised funding and other program features (e.g., AUs, properties), the agreement lacks the mutual exchange of value necessary for enforcement .
   - **Legal Doctrine:** A contract is unenforceable if one party fails to deliver the value promised, as there is no valid consideration.

---

#### **5. Consumer Protection Violations**
   - Misrepresentations during the sales process and failure to deliver services likely violate the **Federal Trade Commission Act** and state laws prohibiting deceptive and unfair trade practices .

---

### **Proposed Next Steps**
Based on the review, you should draft a final email or legal letter that incorporates the following points:

1. **Fraudulent Misrepresentation Invalidates the Agreement:**
   - The sales process included false assurances of funding, fees, and program deliverables. These statements materially induced you to sign the contract.

2. **Material Breach of Contract:**
   - Key elements, such as funding and property support, have not been delivered, rendering the contract unenforceable.

3. **Unconscionable Terms:**
   - Terms denying refunds and cancellations despite program failures are legally unenforceable.

4. **Regulatory Action:**
   - Indicate your intent to report these issues to the **FTC**, **CFPB**, and other regulatory bodies if a resolution is not reached.

5. **Good Faith Resolution:**
   - Restate your willingness to resolve this matter amicably and request a full refund, cancellation of the contract, and assurance that your credit will not be impacted.

---

2. **Material Breach:** Critical elements of the program, including funding and property support, have not been delivered.

Failure to respond to your communications could potentially be categorized as **willful non-compliance**, **negligence**, or a **violation of fair practices**, depending on the governing laws and the context of your complaint. Below are key considerations regarding whether their non-response could result in consequences or fines:

---

### **1. Willful Non-Compliance**
- **Under Consumer Protection Laws (FTC Act, State UDAP Laws):**
   - If BNB Syndicate or MyAbundant is ignoring valid complaints about misrepresentation, fraud, or deceptive practices, this could be seen as **willful non-compliance** with consumer protection laws.
   - Example: The **FTC Act** prohibits deceptive and unfair practices. Failing to address complaints related to misrepresentation or fraud could compound their liability.

- **For Credit Servicers (MyAbundant):**
   - If MyAbundant is subject to regulations under the **Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB)** (e.g., as a credit servicer), ignoring disputes could be a **violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA)** or **CFPB regulations**, especially if disputes are mishandled or ignored.

---

### **2. Negligence or Bad Faith**
- **Negligence in Dispute Handling:**
   - If MyAbundant has an obligation to mediate disputes related to the financing agreement and is failing to act in good faith, this could be deemed **negligence**.
   - Example: Courts may hold companies liable for failing to act reasonably in addressing complaints.

- **Breach of Good Faith and Fair Dealing:**
   - Contracts inherently include an obligation to act in good faith. Ignoring reasonable correspondence could breach this implied duty.

---

### **3. Legal Requirements for Timely Response**
Certain laws impose strict timelines for responding to consumer complaints or disputes:
- **Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA):**
   - If your payments are being processed as part of a credit plan, the servicer (MyAbundant) must acknowledge disputes in writing within **30 days** and resolve them within **two billing cycles** (not exceeding 90 days).  
   - Failure to comply could lead to fines or penalties enforced by the CFPB.
   
- **State-Specific Consumer Protection Laws:**
   - Some states have laws requiring companies to respond to complaints or disputes within a set timeframe, particularly for financial agreements.

---

### **4. Potential Consequences of Non-Response**
- **Regulatory Fines or Enforcement Actions:**
   - Both the **FTC** and **CFPB** can levy fines for non-compliance with consumer protection laws, particularly for willfully ignoring valid complaints.
   - Example: The CFPB has fined companies millions of dollars for failing to address consumer disputes adequately.

- **Enhanced Liability in Civil Court:**
   - Continued non-response could be seen as bad faith, increasing their liability if the matter proceeds to court. Judges may look unfavorably on a party that ignored reasonable efforts to resolve the issue.

---

### **What You Can Do**
1. **Reference Their Non-Compliance:**
   - In your email, mention that their continued non-response may constitute willful non-compliance with applicable consumer protection laws and could result in regulatory complaints.

2. **Escalate to Regulatory Bodies:**
   - File complaints with the **FTC** or **CFPB** if they fail to respond in a timely manner.

3. **Document Everything:**
   - Keep a record of all correspondence and timelines to demonstrate their negligence if this matter escalates to a regulatory body or court.

---

"In addition to the legal concerns outlined in my prior correspondence, I note that your lack of response to my valid requests may constitute willful non-compliance with consumer protection laws, including but not limited to the **FTC Act** and **CFPB regulations**. ## 🤖 ChatGPT

Yes, it may be possible to seek remedies or damages for non-response, depending on the nature of the agreements and applicable consumer protection laws. Here’s an outline of how you might approach this situation:

---

### **1. Legal Grounds for Collecting Damages**
If a company is failing to respond to valid complaints, their non-response could constitute **willful noncompliance**, **negligence**, or a violation of specific laws or regulations, including:

#### **A. Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB) Regulations**
   - If **MyAbundant** is acting as a financial servicer and fails to respond to disputes or complaints, they may be violating the **Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA)** or other CFPB-enforced regulations.
   - The CFPB allows consumers to recover actual damages and statutory penalties, which may include **$1,000 per violation**, depending on the circumstances.

#### **B. State-Specific Consumer Protection Laws**
   - Many states require businesses to respond to consumer complaints within specific timelines. Failure to do so could result in penalties, fines, or other damages.

#### **C. Breach of Contract**
   - If the contracts explicitly or implicitly require communication or a response within a reasonable timeframe, their failure to respond could be considered a breach, entitling you to damages for the inconvenience and financial harm caused.

#### **D. Unfair and Deceptive Practices**
   - Ignoring valid consumer complaints could be viewed as an **unfair or deceptive trade practice** under the **FTC Act** or similar state laws. This could allow you to seek damages or penalties.

---

### **2. How You Can Take Action**
#### **A. File Complaints with Regulatory Authorities**
   - **CFPB**: File a complaint against MyAbundant for non-response to your financing dispute.
   - **FTC**: Report both BNB Syndicate and MyAbundant for deceptive practices and failure to respond to legitimate complaints.
   - **State Attorney General**: File a formal complaint with your state’s consumer protection division.

#### **B. Initiate Legal Action**
   - **Small Claims Court**: If the damages fall within your state’s small claims limit, you can file for the money owed and penalties for non-compliance.
   - **Demand Letter**: Engage an attorney to send a formal demand letter highlighting the violations and damages you are seeking.

#### **C. Explore Refund and Credit Protection Mechanisms**
   - **Dispute Payments**: If payments were made via credit card, initiate a chargeback or dispute with your card issuer, citing fraudulent inducement and failure to deliver services.

---

### **3. Calculating Damages**
To collect money per violation, you would need to:
   - Document each instance of non-response, including dates and correspondence attempts.
   - Quantify damages, including financial losses, statutory penalties (e.g., $1,000 per violation under CFPB), and any emotional or practical hardship caused by their failure to act.

---

### **4. Next Steps**
Here’s what you can do immediately:
1. **File Regulatory Complaints**: Use official channels (e.g., CFPB, FTC, State Attorney General).
2. **Send a Final Demand Letter**: Reiterate your rights, including potential penalties for willful noncompliance.
3. **Track Non-Response Violations**: Keep a detailed record of each violation to support your case in any legal or regulatory proceedings.

## 👤 You

How would it not be hearsay if it was between me and his sales rep Kevin,

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

In a legal context, **hearsay** refers to an out-of-court statement offered to prove the truth of the matter asserted. Whether the sales representative's statements are considered hearsay depends on the circumstances and the purpose for which you are introducing the evidence. Here's why Kevin's statements **may not be hearsay** in this context:

---

### **1. Admissions by an Agent or Employee**
   - Under **Federal Rule of Evidence 801(d)(2)(D)** (or similar state rules), a statement made by an employee or agent of a company can be considered **non-hearsay** if:
     1. The statement was made within the scope of the person’s employment.
     2. The statement concerns a matter related to their job responsibilities.

   - If Kevin was acting as a representative of BNB Syndicate during the sales process, his statements about the program (e.g., guarantees of funding, timelines, or other features) could qualify as **admissions by an agent** and would not be considered hearsay.

---

### **2. Statements Against Interest**
   - If Kevin’s statements conflict with the company’s position (e.g., promising things the company now denies), they could be admissible as **statements against interest** if Kevin were unavailable to testify.

---

### **3. Verbal Acts or Misrepresentation**
   - Kevin's statements might not be hearsay if they are introduced to show **misrepresentation or inducement**, rather than to prove their truth.  
     - Example: You’re not arguing that the program guaranteed $125K+ in funding (truth of the statement) but that **Kevin’s promise induced you to sign the contract.**
     - In this case, the focus is on the **fact that the statements were made

## 👤 You

No. I’m talking about Kevin not testifying for himself, and the owner Steven trying to say what Kevin did or did not do

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

If Kevin does not testify and the owner, Steven, attempts to speak on Kevin’s behalf in court, Steven’s statements about what Kevin did or did not say would likely be considered **hearsay** under the rules of evidence. This is because Steven is not directly involved in the conversation and would be recounting an out-of-court statement made by Kevin. Here's why:

---

### **Why It’s Hearsay if Steven Testifies About Kevin**
1. **Definition of Hearsay**:  
   - Steven’s testimony about what Kevin said would be an out-of-court statement offered to prove the truth of the matter asserted (e.g., "Kevin never made those promises").  
   - Under most rules of evidence (e.g., **Federal Rule of Evidence 801**), this is classic hearsay and would generally be inadmissible unless an exception applies.

2. **Lack of Direct Knowledge**:  
   - Steven was not part of the conversation between you and Kevin. His testimony would therefore lack personal knowledge, violating **Federal Rule of Evidence 602**, which requires a witness to testify only to matters they have directly perceived.  

---

### **When Kevin’s Absence Works in Your Favor**
If Kevin does not testify, this creates significant gaps in the defense’s case:
- **No Direct Rebuttal**: Without Kevin’s testimony, the defense has no firsthand witness to deny or clarify the promises he allegedly made during the sales process.  
- **Weakened Credibility**: Steven’s attempt to refute the claims without direct knowledge may appear speculative or self-serving to the court.  

---

### **How to Leverage This Legally**
1. **Highlight the Hearsay Issue**:
   - If Steven tries to testify about Kevin’s actions or words, your attorney can object on the basis of hearsay and lack of personal knowledge.
   - Example: "Objection, your honor. The witness lacks personal knowledge of the conversation in question, and this testimony constitutes inadmissible hearsay."

2. **Use Kevin’s Absence as Evidence**:
   - Emphasize that the most relevant witness (Kevin) is absent, raising questions about why he is unavailable to testify.

## 👤 You

If Kevin doesn’t show up to testify,
Is my word strong enough against a written contract?

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Your word alone can potentially be strong enough to challenge a written contract, but its effectiveness depends on how you substantiate your claims and the legal framework of the case. Courts place significant weight on written contracts, but **verbal agreements and misrepresentations** made before or during the signing process can override or invalidate parts of a contract under specific legal doctrines.

Here’s how your word might hold up and how to strengthen your case:

---

### **Key Legal Principles Favoring You**
1. **Fraudulent Inducement**:  
   - If you can demonstrate that Kevin made false promises or misrepresented key terms (e.g., guaranteed funding, specific program deliverables) to induce you to sign, the court can find the contract unenforceable.
   - Your testimony about these verbal assurances can be compelling if corroborated by:
     - Email or text communication.
     - Call logs showing interaction.
     - Witnesses (if anyone else was on the call or present).

2. **Parol Evidence Rule Exceptions**:  
   - Normally, the **parol evidence rule** prevents verbal agreements from contradicting a written contract. However, exceptions apply when:
     - The verbal statements are evidence of **fraud**, **misrepresentation**, or **mistake**.
     - The written contract is incomplete or does not fully integrate all terms of the agreement.

3. **Material Misrepresentation**:  
   - If Kevin's promises formed the basis of your decision to sign the contract, your testimony about those promises could establish **material misrepresentation**. Courts can void contracts that were entered under false pretenses.

4. **Unconscionability**:  
   - If the terms of the contract are so one-sided that they are unfair to you (e.g., non-refundable payments for a program that doesn’t deliver), your testimony about verbal assurances could support claims of **procedural unconscionability**.

---

### **Your Word vs. a Written Contract**
- **Your Word Alone**:
  - While your testimony is admissible, it is stronger if supported by **evidence**. Without corroboration, it becomes a matter of credibility, and courts may lean toward the written contract unless there is clear evidence of fraud or misrepresentation.

- **Written Contract**:
  - Courts generally favor written agreements because they are seen as more reliable. However, if you can prove the written terms do not reflect the true agreement due to misrepresentation, the contract can be invalidated.

---

### **How to Strengthen Your Word**
1. **Evidence Supporting Your Testimony**:
   - **Emails or Texts**: Look for any communications where Kevin made promises or reassurances.
   - **Call Logs**: Show proof of communication with Kevin during the sales process.
   - **Witnesses**: If someone overheard the conversation, their testimony could bolster your claims.
   - **Behavior Post-Signing**: If the company acted in ways that align with your claims (e.g., acknowledging funding delays), this supports your testimony.

2. **Focus on Fraudulent Inducement**:
   - Clearly articulate how Kevin’s promises directly influenced your decision to sign the contract and how those promises were false or not fulfilled.

3. **Challenge the Written Contract**:
   - Argue that the contract was entered into under false pretenses and that Kevin’s verbal assurances significantly altered your understanding of its terms.

---

### **If Kevin Does Not Testify**
If Kevin does not appear to testify, it can significantly weaken the company’s defense because:
- **No Direct Rebuttal**: Kevin’s absence leaves your testimony about the sales process unchallenged.
- **Adverse Inference**: The court may infer that Kevin’s testimony would not support the company’s claims, particularly if he is critical to their defense.

---

### **What to Do**
1. **Prepare Your Testimony**:
   - Be clear, detailed, and consistent about what Kevin said, when he said it, and how it influenced your decision.

2. **Corroborate Your Claims**:
   - Provide supporting evidence, even circumstantial (e.g., emails, call logs).

3. **Hire Legal Representation**:
   - A consumer protection attorney can help craft a strong argument based on fraudulent inducement and misrepresentation.

4. **Leverage Kevin’s Absence**:
   - Highlight Kevin’s absence to emphasize the lack of evidence contradicting your claims.

---

### **Summary**
Your word can be strong enough against a written contract, particularly if Kevin’s absence leaves your testimony unchallenged and if you can establish **fraudulent inducement** or other exceptions to the contract’s enforceability. However, supporting evidence will significantly enhance your case. Let me know if you need help structuring your arguments or preparing additional evidence!

## 👤 You

What if I can’t prove it and it was all done over a non recorded call?

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

If you cannot provide direct evidence (e.g., recordings, emails, or witnesses) to support your claims about the verbal promises made during a non-recorded call, your case becomes more challenging—but it is not necessarily unwinnable. Courts will weigh your testimony alongside the circumstances and other available evidence, even circumstantial, to determine credibility.

Here’s how you can approach this situation:

---

### **1. Rely on Legal Doctrines Favoring Your Position**
Courts recognize situations where verbal agreements or misrepresentations influence a contract. You can use the following arguments to strengthen your case:

#### **A. Fraudulent Inducement**
   - **Focus on the Mismatch**: Argue that the promises made during the call directly contradict the contract’s terms or were omitted altogether. If you signed the contract based on false verbal promises, it can invalidate the agreement.
   - **Behavior Post-Signing**: Use the company’s actions after the call (e.g., acknowledging delays, failures, or inconsistencies) as circumstantial evidence of the promises made.

#### **B. Unconscionability**
   - Argue that the contract is so one-sided or unfair (e.g., non-refundable payment for an undelivered program) that it is unenforceable.
   - Highlight the disparity in knowledge or bargaining power between you and the company, emphasizing how you were misled.

#### **C. Good Faith and Fair Dealing**
   - Contracts carry an implied duty of **good faith and fair dealing**. If the company made verbal promises they never intended to fulfill, it violates this duty and undermines the contract’s enforceability.

#### **D. Lack of Integration**
   - Argue that the contract fails to fully represent the agreement because the verbal promises were integral but excluded.

---

### **2. Leverage Circumstantial Evidence**
Even without direct proof, you can provide circumstantial evidence to support your claims:

- **Timeline of Events**:  
  - Show how soon after the sales call you signed the contract, demonstrating reliance on what was said.
  - Detail how the company failed to deliver on the promised outcomes (e.g., funding, program deliverables) after you enrolled.

- **Company's Admissions**:  
  - If they made statements acknowledging delays, miscommunication, or discrepancies after you signed, use these to suggest their prior knowledge of unfulfilled promises.

- **Behavior Patterns**:  
  - If the company or its representatives made similar promises to others, you could use that as evidence of a broader pattern of misrepresentation (e.g., consumer complaints, reviews).

---

### **3. Strengthen Your Testimony**
Your credibility becomes critical in the absence of direct evidence. Courts weigh the **credibility of parties** when determining whether verbal promises were made.

- **Be Consistent**: Ensure your account of the promises made during the call is detailed and consistent over time.
- **Provide Context**: Explain why you relied on these promises and how they directly influenced your decision to sign.
- **Anticipate Counterarguments**: Be prepared for the company to argue that the written contract supersedes verbal agreements. Focus on how the promises materially induced you to sign despite the written terms.

---

### **4. Use Kevin’s Absence Strategically**
If Kevin does not testify, the lack of his direct rebuttal strengthens your credibility:
- **Adverse Inference**: Argue that Kevin’s absence implies his testimony would not support the company’s case.
- **Focus on Accountability**: Emphasize that Kevin was the representative who made the promises, and his absence leaves your testimony uncontested.

---

### **5. Seek Supporting Witnesses or Complaints**
Even if you don’t have a recording, other forms of corroboration can help:
- **Other Customers**: Look for similar complaints or experiences from other customers who may have been misled in the same way.
- **Employee Testimony**: If a former employee or sales representative can testify to common practices or scripts used during sales calls, it strengthens your case.

---

### **6. Alternative Resolution**
If proving your case in court feels too uncertain, consider alternative resolutions:
- **Demand Letter**: Use a formal legal letter to push for resolution without litigation. The threat of regulatory complaints or court proceedings can motivate action.
- **Regulatory Complaints**: File complaints with the **FTC**, **CFPB**, or your **state Attorney General** to prompt external investigations.

---

### **7. Final Options**
If the case proceeds to court without direct evidence, your best approach is to:
1. Rely on your testimony’s consistency and credibility.
2. Highlight the company’s inability to produce Kevin or any evidence to refute your claims.
3. Argue that the company’s failure to deliver on the promises aligns with your account of the call.

---

### **Summary**
Without direct evidence, your case will rely on your testimony, circumstantial evidence, and legal arguments like **fraudulent inducement** and **unconscionability**. While a written contract typically carries more weight, gaps in delivery or Kevin’s absence can tilt the scales in your favor. ## 👤 You

What does good faith mean in terms of law contracts and cancellation

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

In contract law, **good faith** refers to the obligation of each party to act honestly, fairly, and in accordance with the agreed-upon terms of the contract. It ensures that neither party undermines the purpose of the agreement or prevents the other party from receiving the benefit of the bargain.

Here’s how **good faith** applies specifically to contracts and cancellation:

---

### **Good Faith in Contracts**
1. **Implied Duty of Good Faith and Fair Dealing**:
   - Most contracts, whether explicit or implied, include a duty of **good faith and fair dealing**. This means:
     - Each party must fulfill their contractual obligations honestly and without intent to defraud or mislead the other party.
     - Neither party may interfere with the other party's ability to enjoy the benefits of the contract.

2. **Honesty in Performance**:
   - Good faith requires honesty in the execution of the contract's terms. For example:
     - Providing accurate information.
     - Avoiding actions that deliberately hinder the performance of the agreement.

3. **Prohibiting Unfair Practices**:
   - Parties cannot use loopholes, technicalities, or intentional delays to harm the other party or avoid fulfilling their obligations.

---

### **Good Faith in Contract Cancellation**
When it comes to cancellation, **good faith** governs how parties approach disputes or terminations. It requires that:
1. **The Terms Are Enforced Fairly**:
   - A party seeking to enforce cancellation terms must do so reasonably and in line with what was agreed.
   - For example, if a party has a right to cancel, the other party cannot block that right unfairly.

2. **Misrepresentation and Fraud**:
   - If one party induces the other to sign a contract through false statements or omissions, this violates good faith.
   - Courts may find that the contract is voidable if it was entered under false pretenses.

3. **Communication and Resolution**:
   - Both parties must attempt to resolve disputes honestly and fairly before escalating to litigation or other actions.
   - Failure to respond to reasonable requests for clarification, cancellation, or refund could be seen as a breach of good faith.

4. **Unconscionable Terms**:
   - Good faith prevents enforcing terms that are so one-sided or unfair that they shock the conscience (e.g., non-refundable payments for undelivered services).

---

### **Examples of Breach of Good Faith in Cancellation**
1. **Failure to Respond**:
   - Ignoring cancellation requests or disputes in a timely manner can be considered a breach of good faith, particularly if one party is left at a disadvantage.

2. **Enforcing Terms Contrary to Verbal Agreements**:
   - Insisting on terms that contradict promises made during the sales process (e.g., guaranteed funding) may violate the duty of good faith.

3. **Refusing to Refund Despite Failure of Consideration**:
   - If the other party has not delivered what was promised (e.g., services or products), continuing to enforce the contract is a breach of good faith.

---

### **Good Faith in Consumer Protections**
- **Federal and State Laws**:
   - Many consumer protection laws (e.g., **FTC Act**, **state unfair trade practices laws**) incorporate the principle of good faith, requiring businesses to act honestly and fairly in their dealings with consumers.
   - Misrepresentation, predatory terms, or failure to respond to disputes may be viewed as violations of good faith.

---

### **How Good Faith Supports Your Case**
1. **Misrepresentation Violates Good Faith**:
   - If BNB Syndicate made false promises that induced you to sign the contract, they violated their duty of good faith.
   
2. **Non-Response by MyAbundant**:
   - Their failure to address your cancellation requests could be seen as a breach of good faith, especially if they knowingly enforce a predatory agreement.

3. **Predatory or One-Sided Terms**:
   - If the contract terms unfairly benefit one party at the expense of the other, it strengthens your argument that the agreement was not entered into in good faith.

---

### **Conclusion**
In the context of your situation, **good faith** applies to:
- Honest communication about cancellation and refunds.
- Responding to reasonable requests for resolution.
- Avoiding enforcement of misrepresented or predatory terms.

If you can demonstrate that either **BNB Syndicate** or **MyAbundant** acted dishonestly, failed to respond, or enforced unfair terms, you may argue a breach of good faith and fair dealing. Let me know if you need help framing this in a formal complaint or correspondence!

### **1. Violations Under the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB) Regulations**
#### **A. Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA)**:
   - The FCBA governs disputes related to credit billing errors and financing agreements.
   - **Violation**: Failing to acknowledge or investigate a billing dispute within **30 days** and resolve it within **two billing cycles (90 days)** is a violation.
   - **Penalty**: Up to **$1,000 per violation**, plus actual damages and attorney fees.

#### **B. CFPB Loan Servicing Rules**:
   - If MyAbundant is acting as a **loan servicer**, they must respond to borrower inquiries or disputes within a specific timeframe.
   - **Violation**: Failing to acknowledge written requests for information or disputes within **5 business days** and failing to resolve them within **30 days**.
   - **Penalty**: Fines can range from **$5,000 per day for minor violations** to **$25,000 per day for reckless violations**, and up to **$1 million per day for knowing violations**.

---

### **2. Violations of the Federal Trade Commission Act (FTC Act)**
   - The **FTC Act** prohibits unfair or deceptive practices in commerce.
   - **Violation**: Continuing to enforce a contract tied to fraudulent inducement or misrepresentation and failing to address disputes could be considered an **unfair or deceptive act**.
   - **Penalty**: Civil penalties of up to **$50,120 per violation** (as of 2023, adjusted annually for inflation).

---

### **3. Breach of Good Faith and Fair Dealing**
   - Contracts include an implied duty of **good faith and fair dealing** under common law.
   - **Violation**: Ignoring reasonable requests for resolution and failing to respond in a timely manner breaches this duty, especially if the contract terms were already misrepresented.
   - **Remedy**: You may recover actual damages, restitution, or even punitive damages if bad faith is egregious.

---

### **4. Violations of State Unfair and Deceptive Trade Practices Laws**
Most states have laws prohibiting **unfair or deceptive acts or practices (UDAP)**. These laws often cover:
   - Misrepresentation during the sales process.
   - Failing to respond to consumer complaints.
   - Enforcing unfair terms.
   
   **Violation**:
   - MyAbundant’s failure to respond may constitute a violation if it results in harm or prevents you from canceling a predatory contract.
   - **Penalties**: State penalties typically range from **$500 to $5,000 per violation**, plus damages and attorney fees.

---

### **5. Potential Negligence or Recklessness**
   - If MyAbundant is ignoring communications as a deliberate or reckless strategy to enforce the contract, it could face:
     - Claims of **negligence** for failing to perform reasonable duties.
     - Claims of **recklessness** for knowingly disregarding their obligations.

---

### **6. Credit Reporting-Related Violations**
If MyAbundant reports the financing agreement to credit bureaus and refuses to address disputes, it may violate:
   - **Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA)**:
     - **Violation**: Failing to investigate disputes or providing inaccurate information to credit bureaus.
     - **Penalty**: Up to **$1,000 per violation**, plus punitive damages if the violation is willful.

---

### **7. Violations of Unconscionable Contract Terms**
   - **Procedural Unconscionability**: If the contract terms were misrepresented and not fairly negotiated.
   - **Substantive Unconscionability**: If the contract imposes extreme hardship (e.g., non-refundable terms despite failure to deliver promised services).
   - **Remedy**: Courts may void the contract and award restitution.

---

### **Total Fineable Violations**
1. **Failure to Respond**: Each instance of non-response to a call, email, or written request may count as a violation under CFPB, FTC, or state UDAP laws.
2. **Enforcement of a Fraudulent Contract**: Continuing to enforce a contract tied to misrepresentation may multiply the penalties under FTC and state laws.
3. **Failure to Address Billing Disputes**: Could lead to FCBA penalties for each billing cycle the dispute remains unresolved.
4. **Credit Reporting Violations**: If they report the financing and fail to address disputes, it adds FCRA violations.

---

### **What to Do Next**
1. **File Complaints**:
   - **CFPB**: Highlight non-response, fraudulent inducement, and potential UDAP violations.
   - **FTC**: Focus on deceptive practices tied to the contract.
   - **State Attorney General**: Emphasize unfair practices and refusal to respond.
   
2. **Include These Violations in Legal Correspondence**:
   - Use formal language to highlight their regulatory exposure and potential fines.

3. **Document Non-Response**:
   - Record every instance of ignored communication to demonstrate a pattern of non-compliance.

---

Let me know if you’d like assistance drafting your regulatory complaints or correspondence!

## 🤖 ChatGPT

You may be entitled to **damages and potentially statutory penalties**, depending on the specific legal violations MyAbundant has committed, particularly if they have failed to meet their legal obligations under consumer protection laws. While it's clear that much of the misrepresentation originated with BNB Syndicate, MyAbundant is the financial entity collecting payments, and their lack of response is a serious issue.

---

### **Potential Entitlements**

1. **Refund of Payments Made**:
   - You are **entitled to demand a full refund of payments made to date** because the financing agreement is tied to misrepresentation and fraudulent inducement from BNB Syndicate.
   - If they continue to collect payments without addressing your dispute, it could be considered **unjust enrichment** on their part.

2. **Statutory Penalties**:
   - Under laws like the **Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA)** and **CFPB regulations**, loan servicers and payment processors are required to respond to disputes and investigate in a timely manner.
   - Failure to respond could subject them to **penalties of up to $1,000 per violation** (e.g., each ignored communication).
   - Depending on state laws, additional penalties may apply for noncompliance or negligence.

3. **Actual Damages**:
   - You may be entitled to compensation for any **financial harm** caused by their failure to respond (e.g., inability to resolve the contract or cancel payments).

4. **Punitive Damages**:
   - If their behavior is found to be willfully negligent or in bad faith, courts may award punitive damages to deter similar conduct.

5. **Emotional or Practical Harm**:
   - While more challenging to prove, you could argue for damages related to stress, time lost, and the inconvenience caused by their lack of response.

---

### **Steps You Can Take**

1. **File a Regulatory Complaint**:
   - File a complaint with the **Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB)** and explicitly describe:
     - The non-response from MyAbundant despite multiple attempts to contact them.
     - The payments collected under a misrepresented agreement.
     - The undue financial and emotional harm caused by their inaction.
   - This may compel MyAbundant to respond, as regulatory bodies investigate consumer complaints seriously.

2. **Send a Final Demand Letter**:
   - Send MyAbundant a professionally written demand letter outlining their violations, your rights, and your request for:
     - Immediate termination of the agreement.
     - A full refund of all payments made to date.
     - Written assurance that your credit will not be impacted.
   - Highlight that their continued non-response could result in legal consequences or regulatory action.

3. **Explore Legal Remedies**:
   - If they continue to ignore you, you may consider filing a **small claims court case** or consulting an attorney for larger claims. Given the payments already taken, small claims court could be a cost-effective option for seeking restitution and penalties.

4. **Maintain Documentation**:
   - Keep detailed records of all communication attempts (emails, call logs, payment receipts). These will be crucial for any regulatory complaint or legal action.

---

### **Balancing Accountability**

You’re understandably conflicted because MyAbundant’s behavior feels more like neglect or indifference compared to the outright misrepresentation from BNB Syndicate. However, as the entity holding your money, MyAbundant has a clear responsibility to act in **good faith** and address disputes. By failing to do so, they are complicit in the harm caused by the misrepresented agreement.

Filing complaints or taking legal action doesn’t have to feel personal—it’s about holding companies accountable for their obligations and protecting yourself from further harm. You’ve already shown patience by giving them time to respond; escalating through formal channels is a reasonable next step.

Let me know if you’d like help drafting a demand letter, complaint, or other correspondence to move this forward!

> breaches of contract, misrepresentation of funding capabilities, and failure to deliver critical

> could not otherwise afford. After a month of waiting, the funding team has confirmed that I will
> receive far less than promised, with additional delays and no guarantee of success.
> 
> Key Discrepancies and Issues
> 
> 1. Failure to Deliver Promised Funding:
> ○ What Was Said: I was repeatedly assured before, during, and after the sale that
> 
> the funding team would secure $125K+ in 0% business funding within days,
> enabling me to pay for the program and start the business.
> 
> ○ What Was Delivered:
> ■ It has now been a full month with no funding secured.
> ■ The funding team has indicated an additional 30–60 days are required to
> 
> potentially secure a fraction of the originally promised amount.
> ■ Even if funding is obtained, it is unlikely to exceed a small portion of the
> 
> promised amount, insufficient to cover the course cost and to arbitrage
> more than one property as per the program’s methodology.
> 
> ○ This discrepancy directly invalidates the purpose of the agreement, as funding
> was the only means for me to afford the program and arbitrage multiple
> properties, which I made clear from the outset.
> 
> 
> 
> 2. Property Acquisition Process:
> ○ What Was Said: During the sales process, it was presented that properties
> 
> would be provided to students as part of the program.
> ○ What Was Delivered: After enrolling, I learned that I must personally cold
> 
> outreach to landlords and close them into contractual agreement—an entirely
> different and more demanding process that was not disclosed upfront.
> 
> 3. Business Bank Account Setup Issues:
> ○ Following the exact guidance provided—reporting $450K annual income as
> 
> actual, not projected via a brand new LLC—resulted in multiple denials.
> ○ This guidance was impractical for a new business without revenue and has
> 
> caused unnecessary complications in establishing critical financial infrastructure.
> 4. Funding Fees and Timeline:
> 
> ○ What Was Said: I was told funding fees would be 3–5% and funding would be
> available within days.
> 
> ○ What Was Delivered: The process now requires an additional 30–60 days to
> begin, and fees are quoted at 10%, which is double the maximum promised rate.
> 
> 5. Authorized User (AU) Accounts:
> ○ What Was Said: Multiple AUs would be available at $100–$300 each, with

> permanent reporting and only a small recurring annual fee.
> ○ What Was Delivered: I was offered only 2 AUs, costing approximately $500,
> 
> with reporting limited to 60 days.

> ○ What Was Said: My total agreement was reduced to $10,000, with an option to
> avoid fees by paying in full, reaffirmed multiple times.
> 
> ○ What Was Delivered: The financing partner reflects a total cost of $13,000,

> 
> 7. Program Transparency:
> ○ The contracts reference multiple LLCs, including Luvelle Luxe LLC and
> 
> Abundant Servicing, LLC, with conflicting terms, creating confusion about the
> responsibilities and enforceability of the agreement.
> 
> Legal Basis for Termination and Refund
> 
> 1. Material Breach:
> ○ Failure to deliver critical elements, such as the promised $125K+ in 0% funding
> 
> within days and other program features, constitutes a material breach of the
> agreement, voiding its enforceability.
> 
> 2. Fraudulent Inducement:
> ○ Misrepresentations made during the sales process—regarding funding, fees,
> 
> AUs, and property acquisition—induced me into signing the agreement. This
> invalidates my consent to the contract.
> 
> 3. Unconscionability:
> 
> 
> 
> ○ Terms prohibiting cancellation or refunds despite the program’s inability to deliver
> essential promised services are unconscionable and legally unenforceable.
> 
> 4. Failure of Consideration:
> ○ The lack of promised services and deliverables nullifies the mutual exchange of
>
